Three sibling leaks rode along: the runs delta-sync watermark (`runsLastFetchedAt`) was device-global, so B's first fetch took the delta path against A's timestamp and B's older history never hydrated on the device; `SharedPrefsSettingsCache.dropUser` was dead code, so prior users' cached bags — `privacy_zones` home/work coordinates included — sat on disk forever (web already called its counterpart on logout); and the nutrition water-tracker key was per-day but not per-user on both platforms. Sign-out now runs `Preferences.resetAccountScopedPrefs()` (bag mirrors → defaults, goals cleared, watermark cleared — genuinely device-scoped state like theme/locale/onboarded/one-time hints stays), `onSignedOut(priorUserId:)` drops the departing account's cached bags (main.dart remembers the last session's user id, since the signedOut event carries none), and the water key is user-scoped on web + mobile. Pinned by the `onSignedOut account reset` group in `settings_sync_test.dart`.

## 252. Identity-bearing mobile screens subscribe to auth transitions via `ApiClient.authUserChanges` + the `AuthChangeAware` mixin

**Decided (2026-07-15, issues #223/#232).** The §248–251 work made sign-out wipe/reset the *data* layer, but no mobile *screen* listened to auth state — the only `onAuthStateChange` listener (main.dart) handles stores and bridges. Keep-alive tab pages and long-mounted routes therefore kept rendering the previous session's identity: the You tab showed the departed user's email and routed "Your profile" to their profile, the dashboard pinned the prior user's PRs / plan overview / nutrition targets (page 0 of the never-torn-down PageView), the account screen kept A's avatar and coach-consent tile for B (the tile even rendered while signed out), and the setup-wizard gate fired once per process so a post-launch signup never saw the wizard. Per-screen `setState` plumbing from the sign-out button was rejected — any future sign-out path (session expiry, account deletion, deep-linked sign-in) would regress it. Instead `ApiClient` exposes `authUserChanges` (the `onAuthStateChange` stream mapped to the session user id, empty-stream offline-safe like the `userId`/`userEmail` getters), and the `AuthChangeAware` mixin (`lib/auth_change_aware.dart`) is the one seam screens mix in: it subscribes on mount, compares the authoritative `ApiClient.userId` against the id last seen — so the stream payload is advisory, test fakes stay consistent by overriding `userId` + emitting a tick, and same-user token refreshes dedupe away — and calls `onAuthUserChanged` for the screen to clear + refetch. Consumers: `YouScreen` (rebuild), `DashboardScreen` (drop `_serverPbs`/`_planOverview`/`_nutritionTargets` + refetch), `SettingsAccountScreen` (reload avatar + coach consent; consent tile additionally session-gated), `HomeScreen` (re-arm the wizard gate per identity — the server-side `onboarded_at` check keeps it one-shot per user). New identity-bearing screens should adopt the mixin rather than caching auth-derived state in `initState`.

## 253. Apple sign-in on Android is an env-provisioned fail-closed gate, and every auth failure is classified before it reaches a user

**Decided (2026-07-16, issues #241 / #242 / #243).** Three unrelated-looking auth complaints turned out to share one shape: the auth layer handed the user whatever the SDK said, unfiltered. (1) The Android Apple button called `getAppleIDCredential` without `webAuthenticationOptions`, which `sign_in_with_apple` 8.x hard-requires — it threw before any UI opened, so the button could never succeed on Android, only on iOS where the native flow needs no configuration. It now gates on `appleSignInAvailable()`, which reads `APPLE_SERVICE_CLIENT_ID` + `APPLE_REDIRECT_URI` and fails closed with the same coming-soon treatment as the existing `GOOGLE_WEB_CLIENT_ID` gate, so an unprovisioned build hides the button rather than offering one that always throws. The gate dispatches on `defaultTargetPlatform`, not `Platform.isIOS`, so host-run widget tests (which report `TargetPlatform.android`) actually exercise the Android path. (2) Raw `err.message` reached the banner on both platforms — unlocalized developer jargon ("AuthApiException…", "Failed to fetch") that collapsed "that email already has an account", "confirm your email first", "you're rate limited" and "you're offline" into one undiagnosable string. `classifyAuthError` (web `auth_errors.ts` ↔ mobile `auth_error.dart`, kept branch-for-branch in sync) maps an error to a kind and the kind to an i18n key. Classification is duck-typed on `code` + `status` with a message-text fallback, because plain `TypeError: Failed to fetch` carries neither. An `emailNotConfirmed` result surfaces a resend-confirmation affordance — previously a dead end. Sign-up success *without* a session is treated as check-your-email rather than a silent non-event, and deliberately shares copy with GoTrue's obfuscated duplicate-signup response so the flow can't be used to enumerate accounts. (3) The minimum password length disagreed three ways: web's `minlength="6"`, mobile validating nothing before submit, and GoTrue's own default of 6. It is now one constant per surface (`PASSWORD_MIN_LENGTH` ↔ `kPasswordMinLength`, both 8) plus `minimum_password_length = 8` in `config.toml` so the server rejects what the clients reject. **Prod is not covered by `config.toml`** — it reads the dashboard's Auth settings, so raising the prod floor is a manual step, and the constants carry that warning.

## 254. Share-page chrome lives in one `SharePageShell`, not copy-pasted per surface

**Decided (2026-07-17, issue #247 follow-up).** The nine `/share/*` pages plus `/recap/share/[id]` each hand-rolled the identical shell-less navigation — a `.share-header` with the Threkir logo link to `/` and a `.share-footer` with the home + sign-in links — because the share routes are shell-less by design (`+layout.svelte` `isShellless`) and must therefore carry their own way back to the site. That duplication is exactly how the badge page shipped without any of it (#247): a new surface copies a sibling and misses a piece. The chrome is now one component, `apps/web/src/lib/components/SharePageShell.svelte`, that each page wraps its content in via a `children` snippet; it renders the same markup + classes the pages had, so the existing Playwright selectors (`.share-logo`/`.share-footer`) and the `badge.spec.ts` chrome assertions keep passing unchanged. The three now-redundant per-page footer keys (`shareRoute.footerHome`, `shareWorkout.footerHome`, `shareSession.footerHome`) were dropped; the shell uses the canonical `shareRun.footerHome` + `shareRun.signIn` (identical text) across all locales. New share surfaces wrap in `SharePageShell` rather than reproducing the header/footer. Web-only — mobile share paths link to these web pages, no twin.

## 255. A beginner walk-run plan's final week is a `graduation` phase, not `race`

**Decided (2026-07-17, issue #273).** `plan_weeks.phase` is a Postgres enum (`plan_phase`) whose values map to the base→build→peak→taper→race arc a goal-race plan runs. The C25K walk-run generator reused the terminal `race` value for its final continuous-run week, so a beginner — who never entered a race — was shown "Race week". Rather than context-labelling a `race` phase differently for walk-run (leaving the stored phase semantically wrong everywhere else it's read), a sixth enum value `graduation` was added (`ALTER TYPE plan_phase ADD VALUE IF NOT EXISTS 'graduation'`, migration `20270420_001`). It's walk-run-only: `generateWalkRunPlan` (TS) / `_generateWalkRunPlan` (Dart) emit it for the final week, the standard generator's `phaseFor` still returns `race`. The value is carried through both twins in lockstep — the `PlanPhase` TS union + Dart enum, the `planPhaseDbValue`/`planPhaseFromDb` round-trip, the `PLAN_PHASE_KEY`/`planPhaseLabel` label maps (`planPhase.graduation` in all six web locales + `planPhaseGraduation` in all seven ARBs, "Graduation week"), and `PLAN_PHASE_ORDER`/`planPhaseOrder` (appended after `race`, so a walk-run plan's phase marker reads build→graduation). `plan_phase` is a real enum, not one of the narrow-union CHECK columns, so the `check_constraint_unions.mjs` guard doesn't apply.

## 256. Mobile wide layouts gate on an 840dp `expanded` class — chosen so the 800dp flutter_test surface stays on the phone paths

**Decided (2026-07-17).** The tablet/foldable layout pass (`lib/adaptive_width.dart` + expanded variants on dashboard / feed / home / plan-detail / run-detail / runs) buckets width into `compact` / `medium` / `expanded` with breakpoints at 600 and 840dp, and every recomposition — the NavigationRail shell, the dashboard's two-column card grid, run-detail's side-by-side map pane, the runs card grid, the centered content caps — keys off `expanded` only. Material 3's own guidance starts "medium" at 600dp, but 600–839 deliberately keeps the phone composition here: the `flutter_test` default surface is 800dp logical, so gating anything on `medium` would flip every existing widget test onto the wide paths and force a mass test migration for no user-visible win (real phones are <600dp logical; 600–839 is mostly portrait tablets, where the stacked phone layout still reads fine). Each screen's expanded variant is pinned by a ≥840dp test plus a default-surface regression test asserting the phone composition survives. The convention (and the ship-both-tests rule) lives in [conventions.md § Mobile adaptive width](conventions.md#mobile-adaptive-width--widthclass).

## 257. Gender is restricted to male / female / prefer_not_to_say — nonbinary dropped end-to-end

**Decided (2026-07-17, issue #220).** The `user_profiles.gender` option set dropped `nonbinary`. Migration `20270422_001` folds any existing `nonbinary` row into `prefer_not_to_say` (the UPDATE runs BEFORE the CHECK narrows — migrating data first is mandatory or the `ALTER … ADD CONSTRAINT` fails on the surviving rows) and narrows the CHECK to `(male, female, prefer_not_to_say)`. The parity helpers that special-cased `nonbinary` (`calories` / `training` gender multipliers, `age_grade`'s binary-sex gate, `nutrition_targets`'s sex offset) already routed it through the same unmodified-curve branch as `prefer_not_to_say` / null, so a migrated user gets the identical calorie / VDOT / age-grade / BMR result — the change is only the removal of a now-dead branch, verified by the twinned tests. The segment leaderboard **gender filter** offers only male / female (+ All); a withheld-gender runner appears under All, since "prefer not to say" is the absence of a stated category, not a competable one. The UI keeps its historical convention of storing null for "prefer not to say" (the empty option) — preferences normalises a migrated `prefer_not_to_say` row back to that option on load. This is also the first nullable narrow-union column to join the `check_constraint_unions.mjs` guard: a canonical `Gender` TS union now overlays `UserProfile.gender`, the `user_profiles.gender` pair is registered, and the guard's parser learned the `check (<col> is null or <col> in (...))` form so the CHECK↔union stays in lockstep. Dart still treats gender as a raw `String` (no Dart enum), per the narrow-union convention.

## 258. The AI-coach context (`buildContext`) scopes every lane to the caller explicitly, never RLS alone

**Decided (2026-07-17, issue #373).** Every read in `apps/web/src/lib/coach/context.ts` that fetches the runner's own data — the active/pinned plan, recent runs, recent lifts, and the 7-day nutrition rollup — now carries an explicit `.eq('user_id', <caller>)` rather than trusting RLS to scope the row set. RLS was owner-only when the coach context was written, but migrations `20261103_001` (coach run visibility) and `20261116_001` (coach plan access) *additively* granted an active human coach read access to their athletes' runs and plans. Because RLS ORs permissive policies, a user who is BOTH an active coach AND uses their own `/coach` AI chat would have had the athlete's runs/plan UNION'd into their own context — and the no-`plan_id` branch (`.eq('status','active').maybeSingle()`) could return the *athlete's* active plan as the coach's. Worse, the Art 9 health-consent gate keys off the *caller's* `health_data_consent_at`, so the athlete's `avg_bpm` (special-category data) would have been forwarded to Anthropic under the coach's consent — a cross-tenant special-category leak. Scoping each lane to the caller resolves both: all data is the caller's own, so the caller's consent is the correct consent by construction. `plan_weeks` / `plan_workouts` / `gym_sets` carry no `user_id` column and inherit the scope transitively through the (now caller-scoped) parent plan/workout ids — filtering them on `user_id` directly would return nothing. `gym_workouts` / `food_log` are owner-only today but are scoped defensively anyway, so the next additive coach-visibility grant can't reopen the same hole. The general rule: **a privacy-sensitive server read never relies on RLS alone once an unrelated feature can broaden that RLS.** Pinned by cross-tenant tests in `context.test.ts` (coach caller, athlete-first fixtures, fail-before/pass-after).

## 259. The queued-jobs partial index leads `(scheduled_at, id)`, matching claim_next_job's ORDER BY

**Decided (2026-07-17, issue #408).** The worker's claim query (`claim_next_job`, 20260609_001) filters `status='queued' and scheduled_at <= now()` and orders by `scheduled_at, id limit 1`. The original `jobs_queued (scheduled_at, kind)` partial index couldn't serve that: `kind` is not the second sort key, so same-`scheduled_at` rows came back in index-`kind` order and Postgres re-sorted them in memory to satisfy the `id` tie-break — a cost that only grows once tier-priority (20260730_001) enqueues bursts of jobs all stamped `now()`. And `kind` bought nothing on the claim path, because the worker always claims with an empty `kind_filter` (`job_worker` `worker.go` passes `""`), so the index's `kind` column was never a probe key. Migration `20270423000001` replaces it with `jobs_queued_v2 (scheduled_at, id) WHERE status='queued'`, so the `LIMIT 1` walks the b-tree in exact claim order with no in-memory sort. Kind-scoped lookups that do exist are served elsewhere — `jobs_dedupe_map_match (kind, run_id)` for enqueue idempotency, and the kind allowlist is a CHECK, not a scan. Plain (non-CONCURRENT) CREATE/DROP INDEX, matching every prior index migration in this repo (the Supabase CLI wraps each migration in a transaction where `CONCURRENTLY` fails with SQLSTATE 25001); the build takes a brief ACCESS EXCLUSIVE lock, negligible on `jobs` because the partial index only covers the tiny live-`queued` set. Index-only change — no generated type diff; pinned by `jobs_queued_index_tiebreak_test.sql`.

## 260. generate-route + osrm-proxy enforce a DB-backed per-user rate limit, not just the per-IP WAF

**Decided (2026-07-18, issue #339).** `handleGenerate` and `handleOsrmProxy` (the two web handlers fronting billed/self-hosted route engines) previously relied solely on per-IP AWS WAF rules (`waf_generate_route_rate_limit` 100/5min, `waf_osrm_proxy_rate_limit` 600/5min). A single authenticated JWT distributing requests across a small IP pool (CGNAT rotation, residential-proxy pool, a few home/VPN IPs) stays under the per-IP cap while each `generate` call fans out up to `REQUEST_MULTIPLIERS × MAX_SEEDS = 32` upstream round_trip fetches — a free cost-abuse vector. Both handlers now also call the SECURITY DEFINER `check_rate_limit` RPC (migration `20260604_001`) with the caller's own id on the SAME JWT-bound anon client they already use for `auth.getUser` (so the `20260616_001` user-context guard `auth.uid() = p_user_id` is satisfied with no service-role key — the web handlers only carry the anon key), via the shared `apps/web/src/lib/routes/rate_limit.ts` helper (the web sibling of the EF `_shared/rate_limit.ts`). Ceilings: `generate-route` 60/hour (heaviest paid path, Pro-only so no free/pro split — caps a scripted caller at ~1920 upstream fetches/h instead of unbounded, generous for interactive building), `osrm-proxy` 1200/hour (one call per waypoint segment, so the ceiling must absorb a heavy builder session while bounding a single JWT). **Fail-closed**, matching `export-data` / `clip-public-track`: an RPC error or malformed row DENIES (→ 500) rather than letting the paid fan-out through on a transient DB blip; a tripped ceiling → 429. The RPC takes an arbitrary `p_bucket` text key so the two new buckets need no migration. `osrm-proxy` stays **signed-in-only** (its header rationale: an anonymous proxy in front of the self-hosted engine would be a free relay) — so it gets a per-user gate, NOT the anon+IP split `clip-public-track` uses; the transport-agnostic handler signature carries no client IP to key an anon bucket on, and opening an anon path would regress the relay protection this endpoint was built for.

## 261. A coach mid-stream failure persists the partial reply — a consumed daily-cap slot always keeps its content

**Decided (2026-07-18, issue #391).** `decrement_coach_usage` refunds the daily-cap slot only when the stream yielded **zero** tokens; a partial answer keeps the slot "because the user did get something". But the mid-stream `catch` in `coach/handler.ts` returned right after yielding the `error` event, before the happy-path assistant-message persistence block ever ran — so the partial tokens streamed to the browser were never written to `coach_messages`. On reload / second device / navigate-away, `loadThread` re-reads from the DB and the partial reply is gone: a free user (1 of only 2/day) paid a slot for an unrecoverable answer. The fix makes the two outcomes symmetric: **content persisted ⟺ slot consumed.** The catch now persists the accumulated partial (via the same service-role `persistAssistant` helper the happy path uses, same 64 KiB cap) when `accumulated.length > 0`, and only refunds when it's zero — so nothing the user was charged for is lost. The partial is stored as an ordinary `role='assistant'` turn with **no partial-marker column**: adding one is a schema change (coach_messages has no jsonb bag), and a truncated reply is honest as-is — it is exactly what the user saw stream, reads back identically, and `regenerate` mode truncates-and-reruns from the anchor regardless of any marker, so a marker buys nothing for correctness. The SSE body was extracted from `handleCoach` into an exported `coachSseStream(deps)` generator taking `persistAssistant` / `refundSlot` / `logMidStreamError` as injected deps, so the quota×persistence branch matrix is unit-testable with a fake `ProviderStream` + spies (`handler.test.ts`) without a live Supabase — the surrounding auth / consent / cap gates stay on the e2e mocked-route path. The CloudWatch `mid_stream_error` metric-filter log shape is unchanged (kept in the handler closure). Extends §110 (assistant rows are service-role-written); the §28 SSE-streaming note assumed the in-flight request *completes* and writes the row — this closes the failure-path gap that assumption left open.

## 262. Coach regenerate/edit fails closed with 409 when the truncate anchor is missing — no more silent duplicate threads

**Decided (2026-07-17, issue #406).** A regenerate/edit coach turn truncates the active thread from `anchor_message_id` onward before re-running (§53). The handler used to gate that truncate on `anchor?.created_at`, so when the anchor lookup returned no row (a stale id from a race with another tab that already truncated the same thread, or the row is simply gone) it **silently skipped** the delete and still inserted a new turn + streamed a reply — persisting BOTH the un-truncated originals and the new messages, a confusing duplicated history with no error surfaced. The handler now **fails closed**: a missing anchor logs `regenerate_anchor_miss` (PII-safe, via `supabaseErrorFields`) and returns a **409 Conflict** BEFORE any insert or stream, so there is no partial write. The web `CoachChat` maps 409 to the localized `coachChat.errorStaleThread` ("this conversation is out of date; reload and try again"), rolling back the optimistic placeholder bubbles. This is a deliberate behaviour change from silent-continue to fail-closed — continuing against a thread we can't correctly truncate corrupts it, and a reload is the honest recovery. The unit test injects a fake Supabase client through the new `CoachConfig.createClient` test seam (prod leaves it unset, falling back to the real `createClient`) to exercise the post-auth branch that the pre-Supabase early-return tests couldn't reach.

## 263. Wear sync classifies a 409 as a permanent skip, not an idempotent drop

**Decided (2026-07-17, issue #404).** `RunViewModel.classifyDrainError` had a `409 → DropAndContinue` branch that removed a queued run on the theory that a 409 meant "already in the DB." That premise was false. `SupabaseClient.saveRun` sets the row's primary key `id = runId` AND posts with `Prefer: resolution=merge-duplicates`, so a same-run retry conflicts on the PK and merges (200) — it never reaches the drain error path. The watch also writes `external_id = runId`, but because `external_id` always equals the PK, the per-user `runs_user_external_id` unique index (migration `20260528000003`) can never fire independently of the PK, and two distinct runs (different `id`) can never share an `external_id`; mobile leaves app-recorded `external_id` null and importers use `strava:`/`csv:` prefixes, so no cross-client collision reaches a bare watch UUID. The only 409 the drain path could actually see was a duplicate **Storage-object** POST on a retry after a partial success (track uploaded, row insert then failed transiently) — and for that case `DropAndContinue` was actively harmful: it dropped a run whose `runs` row was never inserted (silent data loss). 409 now folds into `SkipAndContinue` alongside 400/404/422, matching this file's §17 classification ("permanent (400/404/409/422 — skip row)") — the run stays queued for manual discard rather than being lost. The companion stuck-queue hazard this entry originally flagged as a follow-up — `uploadTrack` POSTing without `x-upsert`, so a retry-after-partial-success re-409s on Storage forever — has since landed separately (issue #455, PR #499): `buildUploadTrackRequest` pins `x-upsert: true`, so a duplicate track object overwrites (200) and the flow proceeds to the idempotent row insert. With both halves idempotent, a drain-path 409 should no longer occur in practice; the permanent-skip classification stays as the fail-safe so any residual conflict is surfaced for manual discard, never silently dropped.

## 264. Offline-first `create*` methods upsert on the client-minted PK so a lost-ack drain replay is idempotent

**Decided (2026-07-17, issue #365).** Every `create*` in `packages/api_client` backing an `OfflineSyncStore` pushCreate (`createGear`, `logFood`, `createGymWorkout`, `createGymRoutine`, `createMealTemplate`, `createSessionPlan`, `createRecipe`) minted its row with a plain `.insert()` on a client-minted id (the §73 no-reconciliation pattern). That is only retry-safe when a request fully succeeds or fully fails — but the common mobile failure breaks it: the INSERT commits server-side, then the connection drops before the HTTP response arrives. `syncWithServer` sees a normal failure, leaves the row `pendingCreate`, and the next drain replays the identical INSERT, which unique-violates on the PK (23505), is caught the same way, and strands the row as "not synced" forever on data that actually saved. `createGymWorkout` was worse: the workout insert unique-violated on retry before ever reaching `_replaceGymSets`, leaving the workout with zero sets. Each parent insert is now an `.upsert(..., onConflict: <PK>)`, so a replay of an already-landed create is an idempotent no-op-update rather than a violation. Children follow the same contract: `createGymWorkout` now always runs `_replaceGymSets` (delete + re-insert = idempotent, so a retry after a pre-sets lost-ack still populates them); session-plan blocks/items carry client-minted ids so they upsert on the PK too; gym-routine exercises, meal-template items and recipe ingredients are server-id'd, so the create clears the existing children (cascade-covering their sets) before re-inserting rather than duplicating them under the no-op'd parent. The fix is a **band-aid-free root fix** — no catch-and-ignore of the unique violation. The change lives entirely in the shared `api_client` (no mobile twin), pinned by a fail-before/pass-after group in `packages/api_client/test/api_client_integration_test.dart` (each create replayed against real Supabase; asserts one parent row, un-duplicated children, and the gym zero-set recovery case).

## 265. Lifecycle-drip onboarding + re-engagement dedupe now excludes `done`; re-engagement is a monthly win-back, not one-shot-ever

**Decided (2026-07-18, issue #376).** `enqueue_lifecycle_drip()` runs daily and re-evaluates persistent cohort predicates each tick. The onboarding + re-engagement dedupe originally excluded only `('queued','running')` jobs, so once a drip drained to `done` the same still-matching candidate was re-enqueued the next day — onboarding up to 4× across its 2–6-day window, re-engagement *every day forever* to an opted-in inactive user (a duplicate-send / marketing-consent bug). The later `drip_first_week` cohort had already added `done` to its dedupe with a comment noting it did so "unlike the other cohorts"; migration `20270423_001` back-ports that to onboarding + re-engagement. Because jobs retention is 30 days (`20260928_001`), onboarding becomes strictly one-shot (retention ≫ its 4-day window), but re-engagement's predicate outlives retention, so once its `done` row is pruned a still-inactive user re-qualifies — deliberately a ~monthly win-back cadence, not the old daily loop. The stricter *one-shot-ever* / scope-to-"no job since the user's last qualifying activity" variant is left as an open product decision; monthly is the safe, established-pattern default. The **streak** cohort intentionally keeps `('queued','running')`-only dedupe — a streak is at risk every day, so its nudge is meant to re-fire daily while the streak lives. Pinned by `lifecycle_drip_dedupe_done_test.sql` (fails on the pre-fix body, passes after).

## 266. The coach plan-context lane is bounded to a now-centred window, not the whole plan

**Decided (2026-07-18, issue #374).** `coach/context.ts` builds the JSON block fed to the model. Every lane caps its fetch so prompt size + per-call cost stay flat as history grows — `recent_runs` `.limit(runsLimit)`, `recent_lifts` `COACH_LIFTS_CAP`, `nutrition_7d` a 7-day window + `.limit(200)` — but the plan lane fetched **every** `plan_weeks` row and **every** `plan_workouts` row with no bound. A long plan (ultra base block, multi-year) or a REST-inserted row (RLS checks ownership, not row count; neither table has a row-count CHECK) then re-serialised its entire week/workout history into the prompt on every `/api/coach` call — unbounded cost, risk of blowing the context window. Both queries now cap at `COACH_PLAN_WEEKS_CAP = 20` (a full marathon block) / `COACH_PLAN_WORKOUTS_CAP = 140` (20 weeks × the 7-day max). The weeks query is **biased to "now"** rather than truncating `week_index asc`: it derives the current week from `plan.start_date` and fetches `week_index >= currentWeek - COACH_PLAN_WEEKS_LOOKBACK` (a 2-week look-back for adherence context) ordered ascending, so the retained rows are the current + upcoming weeks a coach actually reasons about — not a long plan's stale opening block. A fully-elapsed plan viewed explicitly (its last week is before the look-back floor, so the windowed query returns empty) falls back to the plan's final `CAP` weeks (desc + reverse) so the coach still sees the taper/race, never an empty plan. Pinned by a source-grep guard (the `.limit()` can't be dropped) plus an end-to-end test over the chainable stub that asserts the built arrays clamp to the caps.

## 267. Mobile importers derive a run's local `id` deterministically from its stable `external_id`

**Decided (2026-07-18, issue #361).** The Health Connect and Strava-ZIP importers minted a fresh random `Run.id` on every import for an activity that already carried a stable `external_id`, and the CSV importer's 5-column path did the same (only its 17-column form preserved a server `id`). Because `LocalRunStore.save` dedupes only by `id` and `ApiClient.saveRunsBatch` upserts on `(user_id, external_id)` while carrying that `id`, a re-import double-counted locally *and* pushed the new id into the conflict target — Postgres then ran `UPDATE runs SET id = <new> WHERE (user_id, external_id) = …`, reassigning the server primary key (orphaning the original row, or throwing an uncaught FK violation that aborted the whole batch and permanently wedged the sync queue). This made § 65's "`LocalRunStore.save` replaces by `id` locally" idempotency claim false for everything but the 17-column CSV path.

The fix mints the local `id` as a **v5 UUID over the stable `external_id`** (`stableRunIdFromExternalId` in `apps/mobile_android/lib/imported_run_id.dart`, a fixed namespace), used by all three importers; the 17-column CSV / backup form still preserves an explicit server `id` when present. A re-import now maps to the same local id, so the local store replaces the prior copy (no duplicate) and the upsert's `SET id = <same>` never rewrites the primary key. The root-cause fix lives entirely in the Dart importers; no schema change. A defense-in-depth follow-up — teaching the `(user_id, external_id)` upsert to exclude `id` from its `DO UPDATE SET` so a mutating id can *never* reassign the PK — would need a SECURITY DEFINER RPC or a migration (PostgREST's `upsert` updates every supplied column) and is deferred; with stable ids the unsafe rewrite no longer arises from these importers.

## 268. `LocalRunStore.update()` builds the envelope fresh and its caller fails closed on a local-write failure

**Decided (2026-07-18, issue #341).** `LocalRunStore.update()` was the only write path in the store that `jsonDecode`d the existing on-disk run file with no try/catch — every sibling read path (`_readRunFile`, `_readIndex`, `_readSyncedIdsSidecar`, `_readPendingRemoteDeletes`, `loadInProgress`) already guards the decode and recovers. A malformed file (partial write from outside the atomic path, tampering, restore-from-backup) made the decode throw uncaught, which propagated through the unguarded `run_detail_screen` edit call site: the dialog had already closed, `setState` never ran, no banner showed, and the code risked proceeding to push the edited fields to the server while the local copy stayed stale — mobile and server disagreeing. The root fix is to stop reading the old file at all: `update()` overwrites every field (`_v` / `run` / `synced`), so nothing from the decoded map survived, and building the envelope fresh — exactly as `save()` / `saveFromRemote` do — means a corrupt file is simply overwritten with valid JSON rather than throwing. The edit call site additionally wraps `update()` in try/catch and, on failure, surfaces a `showTopBanner` error and **returns before the `api.updateRunFields` push** (fail-closed) so a failed local write can never manufacture a phantom server update. Twinned to iOS; `runDetailEditFailed` added to all mobile ARBs; pinned by a `local_run_store_test` case that writes garbage bytes and asserts `update()` completes (recovers) rather than throwing.

## 269. Public @handle claims the deferred decisions §31 handle item — a searchable, shareable username

**Decided (2026-07-18, issue #465).** Finding another runner was display-name-only. `user_profiles` gains a nullable, format-CHECKed (`^[a-z0-9_]{3,30}$`), case-insensitively-unique `handle` — the public **@username** decisions §31 left deferred ("URL-safe handles get added later … once we decide on a reservation/uniqueness/normalisation scheme"). This is that scheme: **lowercase-canonical** (stored lowercased, so a plain unique is already case-insensitive; a `lower(handle)` partial unique index is the belt-and-braces backstop), **no reserved-word list** (kept lean), claimed only through the `set_my_handle` SECURITY DEFINER RPC — the sole owner write path, which sidesteps opening a client column-UPDATE grant and gives a clean `handle_invalid` / `handle_taken` error surface (the unique index is the race backstop). Deliberately distinct in name from `runner_handle.ts`'s `Runner #ABCD`, which is a one-way *anonymizer*, not a lookup key. `search_user_profiles` now also matches a handle prefix (leading `@` stripped) and ranks an exact-handle match first, but **inside the same privacy filters it already enforced** — `shadow_hidden`, the `discoverable_in_search` opt-out, and the minor-exclusion floor all AND against the whole row, so an opted-out runner stays unfindable by handle exactly as by name. People search additionally resolves a pasted profile uuid or `/u/<uuid>` URL directly (the `extractProfileId` twin), which is safe because it requires already knowing the full uuid — no enumeration surface — and bypasses the opt-out the same way the existing public `/u/[id]` page does. **Email search was explicitly rejected** as an enumeration/harassment oracle (same class as #399/#454); an invite-by-email flow is the only acceptable "I know their email" path, out of scope here. What's *not* built (follow-ups): `/u/@handle` URL routing (the column exists; only search + claim consume it today) and a mobile claim surface (web is canonical per §24 — mobile mirrors search + display only).

## 270. "Runners nearby" is opt-in coarse-location discovery behind a default-off flag, centred on the caller's own stored area

**Decided (2026-07-18, issue #466).** Finding runners near you is the first surface in the product to expose *person*-location, and it sits directly against the app's strongest privacy invariant (privacy zones, track clipping, coarse-only share pages all exist to *not* reveal where a person is). It ships now, fully built, but fail-closed on every axis. **(a) The unflagged, no-person-location win** improves `fetchSuggestedPeople` (web `core/data.ts`) to add a second signal alongside club co-members: co-attendees of the local events the viewer is going to (event_attendees, RLS-scoped to events the viewer can see) — a "runners around you" path that needs no new location surface, ranked by combined shared-club + shared-event count. **(b) The gated coarse-location surface** is behind `PUBLIC_ENABLE_NEARBY_RUNNERS` (default OFF, `social/nearby_flag.ts`), so nothing renders in prod until owner + CISO/counsel sign-off lands (location is Art 9-adjacent) — a **pre-deploy checklist item, not blocked-on-legal missing code**. The design: a new `discoverable_nearby` pref (`user_settings.prefs`, default false, separate from `discoverable_in_search`); a coarse area stored as `user_settings.discoverable_area geography(Point,4326)` — a geocoded *city/area* centroid rounded server-side to 2 decimal degrees (~1 km), **never live GPS** — written only through the `set_discoverable_area` definer RPC; and the `discoverable_runners_near` SECURITY DEFINER reader (migration `20270424000005`) that centres on the **caller's own** stored area (never a client-supplied coordinate, so nobody can probe arbitrary points), requires the caller to be opted in with an area set (**reciprocity** — a lurker can't scrape the opted-in set), applies every existing person-discovery filter (minor exclusion via the canonical DOB + prefs mirror mirroring `search_user_profiles`, `shadow_hidden`, `discoverable_in_search`, `is_blocked_either_way`, AND `discoverable_nearby`), and returns only a coarse distance **bucket** (0..4, boundaries 2/5/10/25 km) — exact metres never cross the wire. The bucket boundaries are the twinned `nearbyDistanceBucket` / `nearbyBucketUpperBoundM` pure helper (web `social/nearby.ts` ↔ mobile `nearby.dart`, kept in lockstep with the SQL CASE); the UI formats the bucket's upper bound with the viewer's km/mi preference so the coarse label stays unit-correct. pgtap (`discoverable_runners_near_test.sql`) pins opt-out/minor/hidden/search-opt-out/blocked/out-of-radius exclusion + coarse-only output + the reciprocity fail-closed; a Playwright spec pins the default-off render gate. Mobile client UI for the nearby surface is deferred (web is canonical, §24) — only the pure bucket helper is twinned today.

## 271. A user-initiated unsubscribe is reversible in-app via a scoped clear RPC; a bounce/complaint suppression is not

**Decided (2026-07-18, issue #392).** The one-click unsubscribe path flips a per-stream opt-in pref off AND inserts an address-keyed `email_suppressions` row (reason `unsubscribe`), which the engagement send handlers hard-block on before every send. `email_suppressions` is fail-closed (RLS with no policy → service_role only), and nothing cleared an `unsubscribe` row — so a user who unsubscribed once then re-enabled the stream via Settings had the toggle read "on" while the suppression silently dropped every future send. The fix is a SECURITY DEFINER RPC `clear_my_unsubscribe_suppression()` (migration `20270425_001`, granted to `authenticated`, `search_path` pinned) called by the web preferences toggle when a stream flips off→on. It is deliberately narrow on two axes: it resolves the address from the caller's own `auth.users` row via `auth.uid()` (never a parameter, so a user can only clear their OWN block, matching the exact address the worker keys the row on), and it deletes only `reason='unsubscribe'` — a `bounce`/`complaint`/`manual` row is a deliverability / abuse signal, not something a recipient toggling a pref may reverse. The suppression row is address-keyed (covers every engagement stream), so re-opting into either stream lifts it and the per-stream opt-in prefs resume as the authoritative gate. Pinned by pgtap `clear_unsubscribe_suppression_test.sql` (clears own unsubscribe, leaves bounce + another user's row intact, idempotent). This does not change the CISO/counsel send gate — re-consent still only reaches a real send once SMTP + the sign-off land.

## 272. Region unit defaults (mi/lbs vs km/kg) are seeded at account creation from the client locale, not just in the onboarding toggle

**Decided (2026-07-18, issue #488).** The `defaultUnitForLocale` mechanism (mi for US/GB/LR/MM, km elsewhere; the `locale_defaults.ts` ↔ `.dart` parity pair) already existed and was wired into the `/onboarding` units step, but it never actually reached real accounts: the `confirm_age_and_terms()` consent RPC — the FIRST writer of a new user's `user_profiles` row on both the email-signup and OAuth-callback paths, running *before* the client auth-store bootstrap — hard-coded `preferred_unit = 'km'`. That locale-blind km then defeated everything downstream (the onboarding `onMount` prefill read it back, and a skipper was never re-defaulted). The server has no browser locale at insert time, so the client is the only party that can evaluate it. **Fix:** the RPC gained a `p_preferred_unit` param (migration `20270424000006`) applied on the **brand-new-row insert only** — the `on conflict` update branch still never touches `preferred_unit`, so a returning user's explicit choice is structurally safe from being clobbered; the web callers (`/login`, `/auth/callback`, `/auth/confirm-age`) pass `defaultUnitForLocale(navigator.language)`, and the auth-store bootstrap create-branch applies the same default as a belt-and-suspenders for any client-provisioned path. The RPC stays backward-compatible (no-arg → km), so the mobile `ApiClient.signUp` caller is unchanged (mobile region defaults are a separate follow-up; the RPC is compliance-adjacent but only the UX pref changed, not the age/terms consent logic — flagged for CISO awareness). Two downstream resolution sites that hard-coded a fallback were also fixed so the seeded column isn't silently overridden: `/settings/preferences` now resolves the distance unit through `effectivePreferredUnit(settings, profile.preferred_unit)` (was `effective(…, 'km')`, which ignored the column and reset a skipper's app to km on visiting Settings), and both `+layout.svelte` and the preferences load derive an unset `weight_unit` from the resolved distance unit (`defaultWeightUnitForDistanceUnit` — lbs for imperial) instead of a hard-coded kg, mirroring onboarding's own derivation. An explicitly stored bag value still wins over every seeded/derived default, keeping the unit fully changeable in Settings.

## 273. Persona wave 2: a GPS-baro complementary filter, phone-set QNH + fuel cadence, persisted GNSS mode, and a mid-run flash checkpoint

**Decided (2026-07-11, PR #155 — the persona batch's wave 2 + its two finish slices).** The wrist-worn ultra-runner personas' wave-2 findings shipped in three slices, weighted to vert honesty and configuration the phone can push. The vert accumulator gained a **moving-gate** (`elevation::VertAccumulator::push` takes a `moving` flag fed from `state::RECORD`), so barometric drift while stopped — a weather front at an aid station, sleep on a col — re-bases the reference and banks nothing; the finish slice then closed drift *while moving* with a **GPS-baro complementary filter**: a slowly-slewed bias pulls the baro reference toward the weather-unbiased (but noisy) GPS altitude, subtracting drift on flat ground and auto-correcting absolute altitude with no phone in the loop — which largely retires the on-device-QNH need, with the moving-gate kept as the GPS-independent safety. The `SET1` settings frame gained `sea_level_pa` (`FLAG_SEA_LEVEL` — the BMP581's QNH calibration finally reachable) and `fuel` (`FLAG_FUEL`, drink/eat interval seconds, so a desert runner's cadence is no longer temperate-locked); the chosen GNSS recording mode persists as a CRC-checked `CFG1` record in a dedicated 4 KiB flash config page one erase-page below the run-store region (slot offsets byte-identical; a garbage/erased page falls back to the default, fail-closed like `recover_slot`); and the 253-point tier-1 flash cap surfaces on the wrist (`Snapshot.track_full` → `! TRACK FULL` on the Distance page) instead of staying a cable-only `warn!`. The second finish slice added the **mid-run flash checkpoint** — the in-progress run written to its slot every 300 s / 60 accepted points (`run_store::checkpoint_blob`, a non-consuming recoverable blob; `flash_store::place_or_update` keeps a run to one slot so the final commit supersedes; wear-bounded at ~1200 page erases in a 100 h run, L4 best-effort), so a battery swap or brown-out recovers a partial run instead of nothing — plus a phone→watch course push (`course_store` `CRS1` wire over a chunked BLE write), MAX86177 ambient/dark-channel subtraction so a sun-railed-but-worn wrist can recover a real BPM, a persistent `DRINK`/`EAT` overdue tag (the DK has no vibration motor — the transient 8 s banner alone was missable), live on-device TurnCue from the loaded course, and Nav breadcrumb auto-zoom.

The wire-format moves are all additive and mirrored: the new settings-frame flags are pinned to the same golden byte vector in the Dart `watch_settings.dart` + its byte-identical iOS twin, `CFG1`/`CRS1` are new records, and the run-store blob format itself is untouched. The batch keeps the established honesty discipline — nothing banks while the evidence is ambiguous, and refusals/truncations surface on the face, not in logs. The complementary filter's and ambient-subtraction's tuning constants are conservative starting points flagged for on-device calibration; the flash / radio / sensor-register paths were compile-only at landing time, with the sim-reachable halves verified later in the Renode pass (§275).

## 274. A five-task batch closes the owed follow-ups: manual QNH re-zero, SoftDevice-arbitrated flash, HR duty-cycling, the §227 page wiring, and honest prior-boot run dating

**Decided (2026-07-18, PR #554).** Each item in this parallel pass was a named debt, driven to closure rather than re-deferred. **Manual QNH re-zero** — BTN3 long-press on the idle face (the one free gesture; a mid-run hold stays page-back, host-pinned, so a run's altitude reference is never disturbed) snaps the §273 complementary-filter bias so published altitude reads the current GPS altitude; the step is never banked as vert, a pending sub-threshold climb is preserved, and refusal is honest and explicit (the fix must be ≤ 5 s old with a plausible altitude; the outcome always surfaces as a transient 2x banner — `SET 1610M` / `NO GPS FIX` / `NO BARO`, the parked baro task answering rather than dropping the request); the `FLAG_SEA_LEVEL` phone rail is untouched. **`nrf_softdevice::Flash` on the `ble` build** — the run_flash backend became a cfg-gated alias (NVMC on the default build, SoftDevice-arbitrated flash under `ble`): reads stay synchronous on both (SoC flash is memory-mapped), erase/write genuinely await the SoftDevice completion signal (no executor spin-block), `sd_flash_write`'s word-alignment need is met by a `#[repr(align(4))]` bounce chunk, SoftDevice enable precedes store construction, no address moved, `FlashSink` deleted — closing the standing "must be swapped before hardware use" caveat in `run_flash.rs`. **HR duty-cycling** — the MAX86177 samples continuously in Performance but 15 s per 60 s / 120 s window in Balanced / Expedition (`watch_core::hr_duty`, windows phase-locked to uptime-period boundaries), `shutdown()` between windows with a wake-time FIFO flush so pre-shutdown counts can't replay, and bounded honest staleness everywhere the reading flows (face blanks to `--` after one full period, zone-time banking stops, track-point `bpm` stamps go empty) — which also fixed a latent freeze-forever HR display on a wedged bus. **`plan_adaptive_replan` glance page** — §227's deliberately-deferred wiring (BTN3 cycle 31 → 32), a phone-pushed view through a clamping setter with an honest `NOT SYNCED` state. **Phone-side prior-boot run dating** — the `flash_store::manifest_at` follow-up: `payloadFromBlob` detects a clamped start offset (uptime offset < the footer's `elapsed_s`, impossible for a same-boot run) and dates the run as ending now-ish (`started_at = now − elapsed_s`) with track points shifted consistently; the pre-existing golden test manifest was itself an inconsistent clamped case and was repaired (iOS twin byte-identical).

The duty-cycle constants are documented as derivations, not measurements — the DK can't measure power — and the saving is deliberately **not** folded into `gnss_mode`'s projected battery figures until a PPK2 exists, so the projections never claim a win nobody has measured. The SoftDevice flash, BLE, and HR shutdown/wake register paths are compile-only until the dev kit lands; the QNH re-zero's `NO BARO` refusal path was sim-verified in the Renode pass (§275).

## 275. GNSS power-down schedules publish-relative — a deliberate departure from hr_duty's phase-lock — ambient subtraction can't launder a pinned ADC, and the first real Renode pass retires the "tooling absent" caveat

**Decided (2026-07-19, PR #554 batch 2).** Three efforts. **GNSS power-down between fixes** — the last "architecturally owed" power lever: while a run is Recording in Balanced / Expedition, the gps task follows each published fix with **UBX RXM-PMREQ** on UARTE0's TX (u-blox backup mode, bounded duration — the self-wake backstop for a lost wake byte, never 0 = an unbounded sleep) and wakes the receiver with the 0xFF RX-activity byte a 5 s reacquire margin before the next fix is owed. The pure host-tested scheduler (`watch_core::gnss_power`) is keyed to the last **published** fix, deliberately *not* `hr_duty`'s uptime phase-lock: the publication throttle is itself publish-relative, so a boundary-locked wake could drift into its own reacquire margin and sit there, never sleeping again — a duty-cycle scheduler must key to the same clock its consumer's throttle uses. A failed PMREQ write leaves the receiver on (L4 — status-quo draw, never a lost fix), and the design is honest under the sim's PMREQ-ignoring feed: every fix delivered inside a sleep window is one the throttle already drops, so sim and hardware publish identical sequences. **MAX86177 ambient-subtraction completion** — the §273 bright-sun path hardened: the init register traffic is pinned write-for-write by host tests (including that the §274 duty-cycle shutdown/wake pair leaves the ambient config untouched), the FIFO drain demuxes strictly by tag, the subtraction is saturating and zero-floored, and a **laundering fix**: a raw sample pinned at the 19-bit full scale *stays* `Saturated` — clipping destroys the pulse before subtraction can run, so a new raw-DC baseline check stops subtraction from turning a flat rail into a plausible read. The LED AGC is wired end-to-end judging the ambient-cancelled corrected DC (so ambient swings can't oscillate the drive) with a one-sided raw-DC ceiling shedding drive to protect clip headroom. **Renode verification pass** — Renode v1.16.1 is installed; the standing "Renode tooling is absent on the dev machine" caveat is retired, and the first full pass over the un-sim-verified backlog produced a verified / not-verified matrix with log evidence at `apps/custom_watch/sim/verification-2026-07-19/`.

The pass proved a verification sweep is a bug-hunt, not a rubber stamp: it caught a real recorder bug — `watch_core::record` had never ported `run_recorder`'s #330 gap re-anchor, so at 1 Hz a 40 s dropout that displaced the runner past the 100 m hop cap froze distance for the rest of the run (re-anchor ported, 1 Hz path only — throttled modes self-heal via their scaled ceiling; two host regression tests; re-verified in-sim) — and a sim-harness bug that had silently bounded every earlier "sim-verified" claim: stock Renode's RTC1 model (3 compare channels, tagged-only OVRFLW) stalled embassy's period counter so `Instant::now()` wrapped every 512 s and froze the recorder 8.5 min in; `sim/NRF52840_RTC_Overflow.cs` + a `watch.resc` re-registration fix it, monotonic verified past 512 s and 1024 s through a 44-min recording. The lesson recorded: a sim-verified claim is only as strong as the sim's own timers. Baro, HR-sensor, BLE/SoftDevice, and power-draw claims stay bench-gated (the sim has no BMP581 / MAX86177 / radio model), and the receiver's *actual* power state awaits the dev kit.

## 276. Supabase token verification goes through a shared JWKS-capable verifier, because the project signs with ES256 and there is no shared secret to hold

The Go worker verified user tokens as HS256 against `SUPABASE_JWT_SECRET`, in three separately-maintained copies of the same `jwt.Parse` block (live hub, `/v1/export`, `/v1/premium/*`). Preparing the live-hub deploy surfaced that the production Supabase project has migrated to asymmetric JWT signing keys: its JWKS publishes a single ES256 / P-256 key and there is no shared secret to copy. Every one of those three call sites would have rejected every real user token, and `LIVEHUB_REQUIRE_AUTH=1` would have refused to boot at all.

`internal/supajwt` now owns verification for all three. It holds whichever key material exists — the legacy secret, a JWKS fetched from `<project>/auth/v1/.well-known/jwks.json`, or both — and hands callers a `jwt.Keyfunc` plus an algorithm allow-list built from what is actually configured. Keeping both paths is deliberate rather than transitional: the local dev stack and CI issue HS256, production issues ES256, and one binary has to satisfy both.

Two properties are load-bearing. The HMAC branch returns the shared secret and never a JWKS public key — a public key used as an HMAC secret is the classic algorithm-confusion forgery, and `TestJWKSOnlyVerifierRejectsHS256SignedWithPublicKey` pins it. And an unrecognised `kid` triggers at most one refetch per `minRefresh` window, so a rotated signing key heals without a redeploy while a flood of bogus `kid`s cannot be turned into an outbound request amplifier.

The fail-closed sentinel moved with it: `LIVEHUB_REQUIRE_AUTH=1` now demands *some* verification path rather than specifically a secret. Since `SUPABASE_URL` is already mandatory for the worker, the JWKS path is effectively always available, which makes the permissive dev branch harder to reach in production than it was before.
## 277. #527's "gym_workouts needs a public-read policy" is rejected: the redaction boundary is already correct, the bug was a client that never took the companion switch

**Decided (2026-07-20, issue #527).** The issue reported that a followee's public gym workouts never appear in the cross-modal following feed, and proposed adding a public-read RLS policy on `gym_workouts` mirroring the `runs` visibility model — flagged for CISO review as a privacy-boundary widening. That diagnosis inverts the actual state. `runs` has had **no** public-read policy since `20260701_001`; non-owner reads go through the redacted `public_runs` view. `gym_workouts` was brought onto exactly that pattern by `20270313_001`, which dropped its naive `user_id = auth.uid() or is_public` branch (it wire-leaked `external_id`, `last_modified_at`, `notes`, and the plan-link `metadata` to any reader on a `select=*`) and stood up `public_gym_workouts`; `20270327_001` added `public_gym_sets` for the per-set surface. So the two modalities were already symmetric, and mirroring `runs` **means keeping the base table owner-only** — re-adding an `or is_public` branch would not symmetrise anything, it would reopen a closed High-severity leak.

The real defect was the companion client switch that `20270313_001` itself prescribed: web moved `fetchFollowingLifts` onto `public_gym_workouts`, mobile's `_fetchFollowingLifts` stayed on the base table. Because an RLS-filtered read of an owner-only table returns an empty set rather than a `42501`, the failure was silent — the feed under-reported with no error anywhere, and the method's doc comment still described the deleted owner-or-public policy, so the code read as correct. **Consequences:** (1) the fix is a one-line `.from()` change plus the corrected comment, not a migration — there is no schema change, no regenerated type, and **no CISO pre-deploy gate**, because no privacy boundary moves; (2) the general hazard is recorded — an owner-only base table makes a missing-view bug fail *silently and closed*, which is the right failure direction but an invisible one, so every non-owner read path needs a source-level guard rather than trusting an error to surface it; (3) the guard now exists on both rails — `following_lifts_visibility_test.sql` pins the follower contract at the database (public lift and its sets visible through the views, private ones not, and the follow edge is deliberately **not** a second visibility tier — `is_public` is the whole predicate, so a follower and a stranger see identically), and the mobile `architecture_guards_test.dart` pins `_fetchFollowingLifts` to the view, matching the guard that already covers the `public_runs` readers.
## 278. Changing a password needs proof of the current one; an account that has none proves possession of the mailbox instead

**Decided (2026-07-20, issue #381).** `/settings/account` called `supabase.auth.updateUser({ password })` directly off the ambient session, so a live access token — `localStorage` copied from an unlocked device, an XSS anywhere in the authenticated app, a token pasted into a debugging ticket — was a permanent account takeover with no knowledge of the current password (OWASP ASVS V2.1.14 / CWE-620). The step-up now lives in `lib/core/password_change.ts` (`changePassword`), which the page drives with two injected effects: it runs the local `checkPasswordPair` first (a typo in the new field shouldn't burn a sign-in attempt against GoTrue's rate limit), then requires a positive `signInWithPassword` on the current password before `updateUser` — a rejection, a thrown network error, and a session with no email all fail identically closed. `supabase.auth.reauthenticate()` + the mailed nonce was the alternative; re-signing-in was chosen because it proves the same thing synchronously, on the surface the user is already on, with no second channel to babysit. **`/auth/reset` stays exempt** — a single-use recovery token mailed to the address on file *is* the proof this asks for, and requiring a current password on the one flow a user reaches because they've forgotten theirs would be absurd. **The no-password case is handled by a second proof, not an exception:** a Google / Apple signup has nothing to verify, and there is no reliable client signal for it (GoTrue does not add an `email` identity when an OAuth account later sets a password, so inferring from `getUserIdentities()` would fail *open* for precisely the accounts that do have one). Instead the section carries an "Email me a reset link" button — mailbox possession is the alternative proof, and unlike the session it is not something the token holder already has. That preserves the documented "set a password so the Wear OS watch app can sign in" path while leaving the step-up itself unconditional. Contract pinned in `password_change.test.ts`; wiring in `tests-e2e/settings/account.spec.ts`, which aborts `PUT /auth/v1/user` so a regression can't rotate the shared fixture user's password. Web is canonical (§24) so the rule landed here first; the Dart port to `settings_account_screen.dart` — a faithful `password_change.dart` twin (making `password_change.ts` a parity pair), an `ApiClient.verifyCurrentPassword` hop over `signInWithPassword`, the same "email me a reset link" fallback, and the byte-identical iOS mirror — landed in the same PR.

## 279. A signup confirmation that lands off `/auth/callback` fails closed in code — a dashboard setting must not be able to drop the Art 8 consent stamp

**Decided (2026-07-20, issue #363).** The signup-confirmation redirect depends on a Supabase **dashboard** setting no code can enforce: GoTrue only honours `emailRedirectTo` when the hosted project's Redirect-URLs allow-list contains it, and otherwise falls back to the project **Site URL**. With a default or stale Site URL the confirmation link lands on `/?code=<pkce>` — where `detectSessionInUrl` still exchanges the code and mints a **live session**, while the `confirm_age_and_terms()` retry and the GDPR Art 8 consent gate (both of which live on `/auth/callback`) never run. The account is then signed in with `age_confirmed_at` / `terms_accepted_at` null: consent we cannot evidence, under which the downstream Art 9 processing is unlawful. The issue was filed as "not code — it's a config item"; the decision is that a privacy-boundary invariant may not rest on a console toggle, so the config item stands **and** the app defends itself.

Two pure helpers in `lib/core/auth_confirmation.ts` carry it. `strayConfirmationTarget` routes a `?code=` (or an implicit-flow `#access_token=`) arriving on any route that does not own one back to `/auth/callback`, query and hash intact, so the callback still performs the exchange + stamp + gate; three routes are exempt because they own their own code (`/auth/callback`, `/auth/reset`, and `/settings/integrations` for the Strava return). The URL is snapshotted in `+layout.svelte` **during hydration**, deliberately ahead of supabase-js' async bootstrap — a redirect decision that reads the address bar after the SDK has consumed the code and `replaceState`'d it away would be exactly as silent as the bug. `verifyConsentStamped` extracts the callback's existing fail-closed profile check (every ambiguity — missing row, null stamp, failed read, thrown error — resolves to `/auth/confirm-age`, safe because the RPC is idempotent) and gives `/login` the same guarantee: with `enable_confirmations = false` a sign-up gets a session immediately, so there is no callback hop on which a silently-failed stamp RPC would be retried, and the previous fire-and-forget `catch` there was a second instance of the same hole. The extraction is justified by that second call site, not anticipated ones.

The dashboard step is simultaneously **promoted from "pending" to a pre-deploy checklist item** in `web_app_auth.md § Production setup` and `releasing.md § Before cutting a release`, per the compliance-sign-off rule (§150): a config gate is verified at every release, not tracked as a follow-up. The code guard is the safety net; it is explicitly not a licence to skip the config, because a wrong Site URL still points confirmation mail at the wrong host.

## 280. New-generation Supabase API keys: header shape derives from the key format, and server code never reads the key env vars directly

**Decided (2026-07-21).** Supabase's `sb_secret_…` / `sb_publishable_…` keys are not JWTs, which breaks two habits the legacy keys allowed. First, sending the key as `Authorization: Bearer` — correct for a legacy `service_role` JWT — makes the gateway forward the non-JWT value to Postgres, where it is rejected; the Go worker's `internal/supakey` (and its Deno twin `secretKeyHeaders` in `functions/_shared/api_keys.ts`) therefore derives the header shape from the key's format: `apikey` alone for `sb_…` keys, `apikey` + bearer for a legacy JWT. The worker's env var is renamed `SUPABASE_SECRET_KEY` to match what it now holds; either generation works, so local stacks (legacy demo keys) need no change. Second, the platform's `verify_jwt` gate only accepts JWTs, and a logged-out caller's supabase-js sends the API key as the bearer — so `clip-public-track`, the one function anonymous spectators reach (public share pages), moves to `verify_jwt = false` with its existing in-handler authorization (RLS via the anon-key client, fail-closed empty result, per-IP rate limit) as the sole trust boundary, same posture as `donations-checkout`. `_shared/verify_jwt_config.test.ts` pins the anon-reachable allowlist in both directions.

Edge Functions read keys exclusively through `_shared/api_keys.ts` — `secretKey()` / `publishableKey()` prefer the new JSON-by-name env vars (`SUPABASE_SECRET_KEYS` / `SUPABASE_PUBLISHABLE_KEYS`) and fall back to the legacy plain-string vars, because the legacy vars keep being injected after migration but serve a stale value once legacy keys are disabled (supabase/supabase#37648). The fallback makes the cutover flag-day-free: every function works before, during, and after the dashboard disable. Don't reintroduce inline `Deno.env.get('SUPABASE_SERVICE_ROLE_KEY')` / `('SUPABASE_ANON_KEY')` reads.

**The `createClient(url, key)` sites deliberately do NOT strip the bearer supabase-js auto-sends.** Sessionless supabase-js (verified in the published 2.110.0 `fetchWithAuth`) sends `Authorization: Bearer <key>` equal to `apikey`, and the docs' "will be rejected as the value is not a JWT" sentence reads as if that breaks — but the equal-values case is the gateway's compatibility carve-out and is **accepted**: tested empirically against this project's gateway (2026-07-21) with the publishable key, `apikey`-only and equal-bearer both return 200 with correct role mapping, while a bearer *differing* from the apikey reproduces the documented PGRST301 rejection. Only raw `fetch` paths (Go worker, `export-data`'s PostgREST query) need explicit header shaping, via `internal/supakey` / `secretKeyHeaders()`. The secret-key path cannot be pre-verified (the CLI masks `sb_secret_` values even in JSON output), so the function smoke-test after the first deploy with `SUPABASE_SECRET_KEYS` present is the gate that confirms it before legacy keys are disabled.

## 281. Live-hub browser subscribers carry the JWT in Sec-WebSocket-Protocol, never in the URL

**Decided (2026-07-21).** The hub's authenticated WS subscribe used a `?token=<jwt>` querystring because browsers can't set an `Authorization` header on the upgrade, and the May 2026 owasp audit (High #2b) flagged that as log-bearing at Fly's edge, gating public cutover on a log-redaction pipeline. Phase F research falsified the premise — an empirical canary test plus Fly's docs show the platform surfaces **no per-request access logs at all** (app stdout only, 7-day retention) — but the transport was changed anyway, at the root: a browser *can* set exactly one upgrade header via the constructor's protocols argument, so an authenticated subscribe now offers `Sec-WebSocket-Protocol: livehub-bearer, <jwt>` (`subscribeProtocols` in web `live_hub_helpers.ts`), the server extracts the entry beside the `livehub-bearer` marker (`subprotocolToken` in `livehub/auth.go`, fail-closed when the marker is absent) and must echo exactly the marker back (`AcceptOptions.Subprotocols` — a browser aborts when the response names no offered protocol, and echoing the token would reflect a credential). The querystring channel is **deleted, not deprecated** — the change landed while zero clients were cut over (`PUBLIC_LIVE_HUB_URL` unset everywhere), so there is no compat shim, and tests on both sides pin that `?token=` is not read and subscribe URLs are token-free. Push (POST) and snapshot (plain fetch) keep the normal `Authorization` header; anonymous public-run subscribes offer no protocols at all. This closes the audit finding structurally: no URL ever carries a token, so no log pipeline, Fly behaviour change, or client-side telemetry capture can leak one.

## 282. Live tracking bridges the two transports; the cutover flips clients only after old and new can see each other

**Decided (2026-07-21).** "Share my live location" has two transports: the legacy path (recorder INSERTs `live_run_pings`, spectator reads Supabase Realtime) and the Go hub (recorder POSTs `/push`, spectator streams a WebSocket). Each client picks its transport at BUILD time — web `/live/[id]` does `if (isLiveHubConfigured()) openLiveWebSocket() else supabase.channel(...)`, the recorder (`live_broadcaster.dart`) swaps hub-vs-`insertLivePing` the same way — with **no runtime failover**. Because web deploys atomically but the mobile recorder rolls over gradually (Play review + user updates), a naive cutover (flip `PUBLIC_LIVE_HUB_URL`, publish `web@*`) would move every web spectator to the hub while most runners are still writing to `live_run_pings`, so a spectator watching a current-build runner sees an **empty map** — silent, reads as "runner not moving." That's a regression on a working feature, for latency/headroom nobody is currently missing, so the 2026-07-21 cutover releases were withdrawn (see reviews/live-hub-and-api-keys.md) rather than approved.

The fix is a **bridge** that makes transport choice irrelevant during the rollover, built in two halves matching the cutover order. The **Realtime→hub** half ships now (`internal/livehub/bridge.go`): a single-replica poll loop over `live_run_pings` (cursor on the monotonic `id`, started at max-id so only new rows forward) republishes each new row into the matching hub room — gated on the run having ≥1 hub subscriber (no rooms for runs nobody watches here) and NOT being hub-native (`Hub.RecentlyPushed`, stamped by the push handler via `MarkPushed`; a run receiving direct `/push` already fans out, so re-forwarding its persisted rows would double-deliver). In-zone "coarse" SAR rows are skipped, matching the hub push path which drops in-zone pings outright. This unblocks the **web** cutover: a new web spectator sees a legacy-transport (old-mobile) run. The **hub→Realtime** half ships alongside it (`Server.persistLivePing`, gated on `Server.Persister` + `Server.RunMeta`): every accepted `/push` is also mirrored — async, best-effort — into `live_run_pings`, so a legacy Realtime spectator (every mobile spectator, since `live_spectator_screen.dart` has no hub path; plus old web) sees a hub-transport run. It resolves the run's `user_id` (NOT NULL) from the same per-room run-meta cache the authorizer warmed, and the Bridge's hub-native guard skips these self-inserts so they aren't re-forwarded into hub rooms. A persist failure is logged and swallowed — the push already succeeded via the in-process fan-out. With both halves live, transport choice is fully irrelevant during the rollover, so **both** the web and mobile cutovers are safe and can land together. The Bridge holds the concrete in-process Hub (subscriber gate + hub-native guard + in-memory cursor), so it is single-replica only; a Redis (multi-process) deploy moves the cursor + hub-native state into Redis — deferred with the rest of the Redis path. Rejected alternatives: web-only dual-read (subscribe both, dedup — half-fix, leaves old-spectator-of-new-recorder broken) and accepting the gap (a knowing regression, defensible only at ~0 live-tracking traffic).

## 283. The live spectator page never synthesises movement, and the follow-cam yields to the viewer

**Decided (2026-07-21).** `/live/[id]`'s demo animation (a fake dot circling Melbourne after 5 s in `connecting`) is deleted outright rather than gated: every path that could reach it required a real, visible `public_runs` row, so it only ever fired on real runs — most visibly right after a runner stopped, because `endLiveBroadcast` wipes `live_run_pings` while the saved end is still inside the 2-minute finished slack, and a reload then showed fabricated movement on a real person's tracking page (issue #603). The state boundaries now live in the pure, unit-tested `lib/runs/live_spectator_status.ts`: a surviving ping backlog always renders live (pings are ground truth); no backlog with the row's end already passed freezes on the saved totals as Finished (the slack still guards only the skip-realtime fast path, since a row can carry a *projected* duration while in progress); anything else holds the honest "waiting for the runner" state indefinitely. Relatedly (issue #602), the per-ping follow-cam now respects the viewer: a `dragstart` (user gestures only) or activating the new `GeolocateControl` latches the auto-`panTo` off, and a re-center-on-runner button — the web twin of mobile `LiveRunMap`'s `_userPanned` + FAB — restores it, so a crew member can read the course or their own position without the map yanking back on the next ping. Map init also moved from onMount's synchronous call (which raced the `{#if mapConsented}` container bind and left banner-consented viewers with a blank map) to an `$effect` on consent + container.

## 284. The watch's BTN3 cycle is filtered — data-present ∩ curated, Dashboard forced — superseding §225's fixed 32-page walk

**Decided (2026-07-21, issue #596).** §225 accepted a long fixed BTN3 cycle (32 pages by the time of this entry) on the grounds that display-only pages are cheap. In practice most of the cycle was blank on any real run — roughly 20 pages render an honest empty state until a course is loaded or the phone syncs a summary — so the accepted trade-off degraded into "~16 presses of NOT SYNCED to reach the page you want." The cycle is now the intersection of three masks, computed per snapshot (`Snapshot::pages_mask`): **data presence** (a page is in the cycle exactly when the data behind its empty state exists; the core run pages and the Back-to-start safety page are always present), the **runner's curated set** (a `u32` bitmask + a hide-empty toggle pushed over the `SET1` settings frame — `FLAG_PAGES`/`FLAG_HIDE_EMPTY`, which consumed the last two version-1 flag bits, so the NEXT settings field is a version bump), and a forced **Dashboard bit** so no mask can empty the cycle. `Page::next_in`/`prev_in` walk the mask (host-tested), the page-dot indicator counts the filtered cycle rather than the enum size, and a page the runner is parked on stays counted even when its data vanishes mid-run. Hide-empty defaults ON but is itself a setting: §225's "every empty state visitable" behaviour is one push away, deliberately preserved rather than deleted. Discoverability rode along: the idle face's title row alternates a `B3 MODE / HOLD REZERO` hint during the post-press animation window (steady brand outside it, so the idle face still never redraws per-second), and the terrain-aware pacer tag + `TRACK 1/k RES` notice follow the same "state machines must be visible on the face" rule. Host-tested + sim-feature-built; not sim/hardware-verified on this machine (Renode absent here — the models live on the Linux dev box).

## 285. Run-store v2: byte 15 becomes a record tag (laps interleave with points) and a full slot decimates instead of truncating; the Threkir GATT service is encrypted-only with one persisted bond

**Decided (2026-07-21, issues #598 + #599).** Two wire-format changes landed together while the fleet is zero watches, so no migration shim exists anywhere. **Run-store v2 (#599):** every 16-byte record's previously-reserved byte 15 is now a tag (`0` = point — making every v1 blob a valid tagged stream — `1` = a closed lap), lap records (`index | lap_distance_dm | split_s | moving_s`) interleave with points in close order via a drained recorder queue (so a throttled-mode fix that closes several laps stores each), and the header version byte bumps to 2 so a v1-only reader rejects a lap-bearing blob instead of misparsing it as GPS points. `recover_slot` accepts 1..=2; the Dart mirror decodes both, maps laps into the registered `metadata.laps` shape (metadata.md § laps), and fail-closes on versions above 2. The slot-bounded writer now **decimates instead of truncating**: on a full slot every second stored point is dropped in place (laps never are, bounded by a 64-lap storage budget), the incoming stream thins to match, and the wrist notice reads `! TRACK 1/k RES` — a 4 KiB slot now always holds the WHOLE run at coarser resolution, not its first ~4 minutes at full. Golden vectors: the v1 vector is retained as a decode-compat pin, and a v2-with-lap vector is pinned Rust↔Dart. **BLE security (#598):** every characteristic on the Threkir service now requires an encrypted link (`security = "justworks"`, Mode 1 Level 2 — run tracks are location history and settings/course pushes are config injection; fail-closed at the SoftDevice, discoverable-but-unreadable to unbonded peers), advertising is pairable with a just-works LESC bonding handler, and the ONE remembered phone's keys persist as a CRC-guarded `BND1` record sharing the flash config page with `CFG1` (either write carries the other record forward). Just-works carries no MITM protection during the one-time pairing itself — the tier-1 face has no passkey UI — and a new pairing replaces the old bond; both are the standard single-bond wearable trade-offs, accepted and documented rather than hidden. The BLE half is compile-only until the dev kit lands (tracked in issue #597); the wire codecs, decimation, lap queue, and page filter are host-tested (`watch_core` 1163 tests).

## 286. The watch page cycle is frequency-ordered in themed clusters, with Back-to-start anchoring the wrap

**Decided (2026-07-21, issue #596 follow-up).** §284's filter fixed how MANY pages the BTN3 cycle walks; this fixes their ORDER, which until now was accretion order (each page sat wherever its batch appended it), leaving mid-race pages like Roadbook / Fuel / Nav 10–14 presses out while rarely-mid-run synced summaries sat close. Because the cycle is bidirectional (short press forward, long press back), a page's cost is its distance from the Dashboard in EITHER direction, so the declaration/cycle order is now: the live-run cluster (Dashboard → Distance → Pace → Lap → Zones → Splits → Pacer), the course/race-ops cluster (Nav → TurnCue → CutoffEta → Roadbook → Fuel), effort analysis (ElevationProfile → RacePredictor → TrainingLoad → DistanceBand), synced training (GearWear → TrainingPaces → Fitness → Readiness → Goals → RaceDay → PlanReplan → PlanAdaptive), synced summaries (Recap → Streaks → RunStats → PrRecency → RouteSimplify → RouteElev → AutoEffort), and **Back-to-start LAST** — the safety page is exactly one long-press from home, and the whole back half is the long-press walk's territory. Combined with the §284 filter the practical effect is strong: an unsynced no-course run cycles ~8 live pages; a race with a loaded course puts every course page within ~5 presses forward or back. The enum declaration order IS the cycle order (statusbar rank math + a test pin that equivalence), which means the settings `FLAG_PAGES` bitmask bits renumbered with the reorder — free while the deployed fleet is zero watches and no phone-side picker UI exists yet; the golden vectors carry raw masks and were already version-stamped by §284's frame change.

## 287. The watch run-state tag is snapshot-honest: PAU is manual-only, a speed-derived stop reads AUTO, and the min-move artifact keeps REC

**Decided (2026-07-21).** The recorder's `Paused` state means three different things (`Recorder::is_moving` documents the trichotomy): an explicit button pause, a speed-derived auto-pause, and the min-move filter's per-fix sampling artifact on a slow climb — a power-hiker at ~1 m/s covers under the 3 m gate per 1 Hz fix, so the raw state legitimately alternates Recording/Paused fix by fix while distance accrues normally. The face rendered all three as `PAU`, so the core persona's climb read as "the watch keeps pausing my run" (the tag flickered REC/PAU every second on mountain_loop's climb legs), and an aid-station stop was indistinguishable from a manual pause that owes a button press. `face::rec_tag` now derives the tag from the whole `Snapshot`: `PAU` only when `Snapshot.manual_paused` (a new field, gated to `Paused` so `stop()` can't leak a stale flag into `FIN`), steady `AUTO` when genuinely below `MIN_MOVING_SPEED_MPS` (it resumes itself — no action owed), and plain blinking `REC` through the artifact. Display-layer only — the recorder's state machine, moving-time math, and the stored blob are untouched. The same change paired the dashboard's NOW row with live GAP (`NOW 5:55 GAP 5:41`), putting the flagship effort metric on the page a runner actually lives on instead of only the Pace glance.

## 288. The watch run view gains a page-grid overview on a third BTN3 tier — hold to open, inactivity selects, the modal swallows every button

**Decided (2026-07-21).** §284 and §286 made the BTN3 cycle short and well-ordered, but it stayed a blind linear walk — nothing on screen said what pages exist, and a far page under a wide mask was still many presses out. The run view now times three BTN3 tiers: release before 0.5 s = page next, release between 0.5 s and 1.5 s = page back, and holding to 1.5 s (`BTN3_GRID_HOLD`) opens a **page-grid overview** (`watch_core::page_grid`) — every enabled page as a unique ≤4-glyph code (`Page::code()`, test-pinned) in cycle order, four per row, with a cursor box on the current page. §286 is retained, not superseded — Back-to-start stays exactly one long-press from home — but adding the tier moved the page-back long-press from fire-at-threshold to **fire-on-release**: the third tier needs the disambiguation window, so page back now costs releasing inside a one-second band, while the grid fires while still held (the grid appearing IS the feedback). The idle face keeps its two tiers untouched: short = GNSS mode, any hold = QNH re-zero, so duration never changes an idle gesture mid-press. In-grid grammar: BTN3 tap steps the cursor one cell, a BTN3 hold drops one grid row (four steps along the enabled cycle, wrapping), BTN4 jumps immediately, BTN1/BTN2 cancel — and all four are **swallowed**: a press inside a navigation modal never reaches the recorder (no pause, no stop-arm, no lap).
